Appendix 0.C
Completeness proof

To simplify the inductive arguments, we will actually prove the following slightly stronger version of the theorem:

Theorem 0.C.1
(Completeness)

We have

\displaystyle{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathsf{\vdash\,}}{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}T}\ \mathsf{Thy}

\displaystyle\text{ implies }\phantom{{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\Gamma}}\vdash_{\phantom{T}}\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\overline{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}T}}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\ \mathsf{Thy}

(1)

\displaystyle\mathbin{\vdash_{{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}T}}}{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\Gamma}\ \mathsf{Ctx}

\displaystyle\text{ implies }\phantom{{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\Gamma}}\vdash_{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\overline{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}T}}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}}{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\overline{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\Gamma}\color[rgb]{1,0,0}}\ \mathsf{Ctx}

(2)

\displaystyle{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\Gamma}

\displaystyle\mathbin{\vdash_{{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}T}}}\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.5}{A}\ {\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathsf{tp}}

\displaystyle\text{ implies }{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\overline{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\Gamma}\color[rgb]{1,0,0}}\vdash_{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\overline{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}T}}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}}\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\overline{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.5}{A}}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\ {\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathsf{tp}}

\displaystyle\text{ and }\ {\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\overline{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\Gamma}\color[rgb]{1,0,0}}\vdash_{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\overline{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}T}}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}}{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.5}{A}}^{{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}*}}\mathord{:}\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\overline{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.5}{A}}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\to\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\overline{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.5}{A}}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\to{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathsf{bool}}

(3)

\displaystyle{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\Gamma}

\displaystyle\mathbin{\vdash_{{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}T}}}\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.5}{A}\mathbin{\equiv}\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.5}{B}

\displaystyle\text{ implies }{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\overline{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\Gamma}\color[rgb]{1,0,0}}\vdash_{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\overline{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}T}}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}}\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\overline{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.5}{A}}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathbin{\equiv}\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\overline{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.5}{B}}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}

\displaystyle\text{ and }\ {\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\overline{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\Gamma}\color[rgb]{1,0,0}},\;{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}x}\mathord{:}\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\overline{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.5}{A}}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\vdash_{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\overline{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}T}}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}}{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.5}{A}}^{{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}*}}\ {\color[rgb]{0,0,1}x}\ {\color[rgb]{0,0,1}x}\,{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathsf{=}}_{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathsf{bool}}}\,{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.5}{B}}^{{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}*}}\ {\color[rgb]{0,0,1}x}\ {\color[rgb]{0,0,1}x}

(4)

\displaystyle{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\Gamma}

\displaystyle\mathbin{\vdash_{{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}T}}}\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{t}\mathord{:}\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.5}{A}

\displaystyle\text{ implies }{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\overline{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\Gamma}\color[rgb]{1,0,0}}\vdash_{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\overline{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}T}}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}}\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\overline{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{t}}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathord{:}\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\overline{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.5}{A}}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}

\displaystyle\text{ and }\ {\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\overline{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\Gamma}\color[rgb]{1,0,0}}\vdash_{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\overline{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}T}}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}}{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.5}{A}}^{{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}*}}\ \color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\overline{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{t}}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\ \color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\overline{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{t}}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}

(5)

\displaystyle{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\Gamma}

\displaystyle\mathbin{\vdash_{{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}T}}}\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.5}{A}\mathbin{\prec:}\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.5}{B}

\displaystyle\text{ implies }{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\overline{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\Gamma}\color[rgb]{1,0,0}}\vdash_{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\overline{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}T}}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}}\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\overline{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.5}{A}}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathbin{\equiv}\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\overline{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.5}{B}}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}

\displaystyle\text{ and }\ {\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\overline{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\Gamma}\color[rgb]{1,0,0}},\;{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}x},{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}y}\mathord{:}\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\overline{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.5}{B}}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\vdash_{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\overline{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}T}}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}}{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.5}{A}}^{{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}*}}\ {\color[rgb]{0,0,1}x}\ {\color[rgb]{0,0,1}y}\Rightarrow{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.5}{B}}^{{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}*}}\ {\color[rgb]{0,0,1}x}\ {\color[rgb]{0,0,1}y}

(6)

\displaystyle{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\Gamma}

\displaystyle\mathbin{\vdash_{{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}T}}}\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{F}

\displaystyle\text{ implies }{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\overline{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\Gamma}\color[rgb]{1,0,0}}\vdash_{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\overline{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}T}}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}}\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\overline{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{F}}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}

(7)

In case of term equality, we strengthen the claim to:

\displaystyle{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\Gamma}

\displaystyle\mathbin{\vdash_{{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}T}}}\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{t}\,{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathsf{=}}_{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.5}{A}}\,\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{t^{\prime}}

\displaystyle\text{ implies }{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\overline{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\Gamma}\color[rgb]{1,0,0}}\vdash_{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\overline{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}T}}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}}{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.5}{A}}^{{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}*}}\ \color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\overline{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{t}}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\ \color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\overline{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{t^{\prime}}}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\

\displaystyle\text{ and }\ {\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\overline{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\Gamma}\color[rgb]{1,0,0}}\vdash_{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\overline{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}T}}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}}\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\overline{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{t}}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathord{:}\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\overline{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.5}{A}}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\quad\text{and}\ {\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\overline{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\Gamma}\color[rgb]{1,0,0}}\vdash_{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\overline{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}T}}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}}\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\overline{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{t^{\prime}}}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathord{:}\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\overline{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.5}{A}}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}

(8)

Additionally, the typing relations
{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.5}{A}}^{{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}*}}
are symmetric and transitive on all well-formed types
A
:

\displaystyle{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\Gamma}\mathbin{\vdash_{{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}T}}}\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.5}{A}\ {\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathsf{tp}}

\displaystyle\hskip 14.22636pt\text{implies}\hskip 14.22636pt{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\overline{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\Gamma}\color[rgb]{1,0,0}}\vdash_{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\overline{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}T}}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathsf{\forall}}\,{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}x,y}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathsf{\mathord{:}}}\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.5}{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\overline{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.5}\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.5}{A}}\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.5}}.~{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.5}{A}}^{{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}*}}\ {\color[rgb]{0,0,1}x}\ {\color[rgb]{0,0,1}y}\Rightarrow{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.5}{A}}^{{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}*}}\ {\color[rgb]{0,0,1}y}\ {\color[rgb]{0,0,1}x}

(9)

\displaystyle{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\Gamma}\mathbin{\vdash_{{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}T}}}\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.5}{A}\ {\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathsf{tp}}

\displaystyle\hskip 14.22636pt\text{implies}\hskip 14.22636pt{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\overline{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\Gamma}\color[rgb]{1,0,0}}\vdash_{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\overline{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}T}}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathsf{\forall}}\,{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}x},{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}y},{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}z}}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathsf{\mathord{:}}}\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.5}{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\overline{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.5}\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.5}{A}}\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.5}}.~{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.5}{A}}^{{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}*}}\ {\color[rgb]{0,0,1}x}\ {\color[rgb]{0,0,1}y}\Rightarrow\left({\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.5}{A}}^{{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}*}}\ {\color[rgb]{0,0,1}y}\ {\color[rgb]{0,0,1}z}\Rightarrow{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.5}{A}}^{{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}*}}\ {\color[rgb]{0,0,1}x}\ {\color[rgb]{0,0,1}z}\right)

(10)

Moreover, the following substitution lemma holds, i.e.,

\displaystyle{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\Gamma},\;{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}x}\mathord{:}\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.5}{A}\mathbin{\vdash_{{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}T}}}\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{t}\mathord{:}\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.5}{B}\;\text{and}\;{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\Gamma}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathsf{\vdash\,}}\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{u}\mathord{:}\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.5}{A}

\displaystyle\hskip 14.22636pt\text{implies}\hskip 14.22636pt{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\overline{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\Gamma}\color[rgb]{1,0,0}}\vdash_{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\overline{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}T}}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}}\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\overline{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{t}[\nicefrac{{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}x}}}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{u}}}]}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\,{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathsf{=}}_{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\overline{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.5}{B}}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}}\,\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\overline{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{t}}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}[\nicefrac{{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}x}}}{{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\overline{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{u}}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}}}]

(11)

\displaystyle{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\Gamma},\;{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}x}\mathord{:}\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.5}{A}\mathbin{\vdash_{{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}T}}}\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.5}{B}\ {\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathsf{tp}}\;\text{and}\;{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\Gamma}\mathbin{\vdash_{{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}T}}}\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{u}\mathord{:}\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.5}{B}

\displaystyle\hskip 14.22636pt\text{implies}\hskip 14.22636pt{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\overline{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\Gamma}\color[rgb]{1,0,0}}\vdash_{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\overline{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}T}}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}}\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\overline{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.5}{B}[\nicefrac{{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}x}}}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{u}}}]}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathbin{\equiv}\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\overline{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.5}{B}}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}[\nicefrac{{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}x}}}{{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\overline{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{u}}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}}}]

(12)

In the following line, we assume that if
\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{t}=\lambda{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}y}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathsf{\mathord{:}}}\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.5}{C}.~\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{s}
for
\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{s}
of type
\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.5}{D}
, then
\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.5}{B}={\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathsf{\Pi}}{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}y}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathsf{\mathord{:}}}\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.5}{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.5}{C}}.~\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.5}{D}
(this is enough in practice and we cannot easily show more). In the presence of nontrivial subtyping we need this assumption that types ”fit together exactly” to prove this part of the substitution lemma.

\displaystyle{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\Gamma},\;{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}x}\mathord{:}\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.5}{A}\mathbin{\vdash_{{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}T}}}\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{t}\mathord{:}\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.5}{B}

\displaystyle\hskip 14.22636pt\text{implies}\hskip 14.22636pt{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\overline{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\Gamma}\color[rgb]{1,0,0}},\;{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}x},{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}x^{\prime}}\mathord{:}\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\overline{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.5}{A}}\color[rgb]{0,0,0},\;\triangleright\,{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.5}{A}}^{{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}*}}\ {\color[rgb]{0,0,1}x}\ {\color[rgb]{0,0,1}x^{\prime}}\vdash_{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\overline{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}T}}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}}{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.5}{B}}^{{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}*}}\ \color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\overline{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{t}}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\ \color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\overline{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{t}}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}[\nicefrac{{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}x}}}{{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}x^{\prime}}}}]

(13)

Here Case
4
looks weaker than in the original statement, but is easily seen to be equivalent.
The equivalence proof uses induction on the shape of the types (reducing the claim to base types), propositional extensionality and the PER axioms.
Furthermore, the statement that
{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.5}{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.5}{A}}}^{{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}*}}
is a PER no longer appears in the statement for well-typedness since the fact that the PERs are actually PERs is proved separately.

Proof
(Proof of Theorem
0.C.1
)

Firstly, we will prove the substitution lemma by induction on the grammar, i.e. by induction on the shape of the terms and types.

Afterwards, we will prove completeness of the translation w.r.t. all DHOL judgements by induction on the derivations. This means that we consider the inference rules of DHOL and prove that if completeness holds for the assumptions of a DHOL inference rule, then it also holds for the conclusion of the rule.
For the inductive steps for some typing rules, namely (
), we also require the fact that for any (well-formed) type
\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.5}{A}
in DHOL we have
{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.5}{A}}^{{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}*}}\mathord{:}\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\overline{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.5}{A}}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\to\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\overline{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.5}{A}}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\to{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathsf{bool}}
.
This follows directly from how the
{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.5}{A}}^{{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}*}}
are generated/defined in the translation.

0.C.1
Substitution lemma and symmetry and transitivity of the typing relations

12
) is obvious.

Appendix 0.D
Soundness proof

The idea of the soundness proof is to transform HOL-proofs into DHOL-proofs.

The key idea is to transform a HOL-proof of
\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\overline{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{F}}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}
into one only using terms in the well-typed image of the translation, at which point we can read off a (partial) DHOL-proof of
\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{F}
.
This DHOL proof will be missing sub-proofs to showing the well-typedness of terms occurring in the proof.
Using the (assumed) well-typedness of the DHOL problem and induction, we can show that all terms occurring in the partial DHOL proof are in fact well-typed, yielding the existence of a complete DHOL proof.
We proceed in multiple steps:

1.

prove that the translation is injective for terms of equal given DHOL type,

2.

define quasi-preimages for terms not in image of translation,

3.

given valid HOL derivation of translation of well-typed validity conjecture, choose DHOL types of quasi-preimages of terms in it,

4.

modify derivation to make terms in it (almost) proper,

5.

lift modified HOL derivation to DHOL derivation.

0.D.1
Type-wise injectivity of the translation

Definition 4

Let
\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{t}
be an ill-typed DHOL term with well-typed image
\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\overline{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{t}}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}
in HOL. In this case we will say that
\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\overline{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{t}}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}
is a
spurious
term w.rt. its preimage
\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{t}
. If the preimage is unique or clear from the context we will simply say that
\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\overline{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{t}}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}
is spurious.
Similarly, a term
\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\overline{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{s}}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}
in HOL that is the image of a well-typed term
\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{s}
, will be called
proper
w.r.t its preimage
\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{s}
. A term
tm
in HOL that is not the image of any (well-typed or not) term is said to be
improper
.

Lemma 5

Let
{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\Delta}
be a DHOL context and let
{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\Gamma}
denote its translation.
Given two DHOL terms
\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{s},\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{t}
of type
\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.5}{A}
and assuming
\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{s}
and
\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{t}
are not identical, it follows that
\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\overline{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{s}}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}
and
\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\overline{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{t}}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}
are not identical.

Proof
(Proof of Lemma
5
)

We prove this by induction on the shape of the types both equalities are over — in case both terms are equalities — and by subinduction on the shape of the two translated terms otherwise.
We observe that terms created using a different top-level production are non-identical and will remain that way in the image.
So we can go over the productions one by one and assuming type-wise injectivity for subterms show injectivity of applying them.
Different constants are mapped to different constants and different variables to different variables, so in those cases there is nothing to prove.
If two function applications or implications differ in DHOL then one of the two pairs of corresponding arguments must differ as well. By induction hypothesis so will the images of the terms in that pair. Since function application and implication both commute with the translation, it follows that the images of the function applications or implications also differ.
Since the translations of the terms on both sides of an equality also show up in the translation, the same argument also works for two equalities over the same type. Similarly for lambda functions
over the same type.

Consider now two equalities over different types that get identified by dependency-erasure.

In case of equalities over different base types, the typing relations that are applied in the images are different, so the images of the equalities differ.
For equalities over different
\Pi
-types either the domain type or the codomain type must differ by rule (
).
If the domain types differ then the typing assumption after the two universal quantifiers of the translated equalities will differ.
If the codomain types are different then the applications of the typing relations on the right of the
\Rightarrow
of the translated equalities are the translations of the equalities yielded by applying the functions on both sides of the equalities to a freshly bound variable of the domain type.
The translations of the equalities are only identical if those ”inner equalities” are identical. Furthermore, the inner equalities are over types that are the codomain of the type the equalities are over. The claim then follows from the induction hypothesis.

Finally it remains to consider the case of equalities
\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{s}\,{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathsf{=}}_{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.5}{A}|_{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{p}}\hskip 1.13809pt}\,\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{t}
and
\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{s^{\prime}}\,{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathsf{=}}_{A^{\prime}|_{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{p^{\prime}}}\hskip 1.13809pt}\,\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{t^{\prime}}
over non-identical refinement types
\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.5}{A}|_{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{p}}\hskip 1.13809pt
and
A^{\prime}|_{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{p^{\prime}}}\hskip 1.13809pt
where not both
\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.5}{A}=\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.5}{A^{\prime}}
and
\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{p}=\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{p^{\prime}}
.
If
\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{p}\neq\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{p^{\prime}}
, then the translations have different subterms
\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\overline{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}p}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\ \color[rgb]{0,0,1}{s}
and
\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\overline{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{p^{\prime}}}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\ \color[rgb]{0,0,1}{s^{\prime}}
and thus differ.
If
\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.5}{A}\neq\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.5}{A^{\prime}}
, then the first conjuncts in the translated equalities are the translations of equalities over the types
\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.5}{A}
and
\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.5}{A^{\prime}}
respectively, which by the induction hypothesis have different translations.
So in any case, the equalities have different images.
The case of equalities over quotient types works analogously.

0.D.2
Quasi-preimages for terms and validity statements in admissible HOL derivations

Firstly, we will consider the preimage of a typing relations
{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.5}{A}}^{{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}*}}
to be the equality symbol
\lambda{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}x}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathsf{\mathord{:}}}\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.5}{A}.~\lambda{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}y}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathsf{\mathord{:}}}\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.5}{A}.~{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}x}\,{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathsf{=}}_{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.5}{A}}\,{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}y}
(if equality is treated as a (parametric) binary predicate rather than a production of the grammar this eta reduces to the symbol
\,{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathsf{=}}_{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.5}{A}}\,
).

Using this convention, we define the normalization of an improper HOL term, which is either a proper term or a spurious term.
The normalization of an improper HOL term is defined by:

\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{t}

(PT31)

\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{F}
not of shape

(PT35)

For terms
\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{t}
in the image of the translation, we define the normalization of
\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{t}
be be
\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{t}
itself.

Definition 6

Assume a well-formed DHOL theory
{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}T}
.

We say that an HOL context
{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\Delta}
is
proper
(relative to
\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\overline{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}T}}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}
), iff there exists a well-formed HOL context
{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\Theta}
(relative to
\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\overline{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}T}}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}
), s.t. there is a well-formed DHOL context
{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\Gamma}
(relative to
{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}T}
) with
{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\overline{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\Gamma}\color[rgb]{1,0,0}}={\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\Theta}
and
{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\Theta}
can be obtained from
{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\Delta}
by adding well-typed typing assumptions.
In this case,
{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\Gamma}
is called a
quasi-preimage
of
{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\Delta}
.
Inspecting the translation, it becomes clear that
{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\Gamma}
is uniquely determined by the choices of the preimages of the types of variables without a typing assumption in
{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\Delta}
.

Given a proper HOL context
{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\Delta}
and a well-typed HOL formula
\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{\varphi}
over
{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\Delta}
, we say that
\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{\varphi}
is
quasi-proper
iff
\mathsf{repl}\left[\varphi\right]=\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\overline{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{F}}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}
for
{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\Gamma}\mathbin{\vdash_{{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}T}}}\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{F}:{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathsf{bool}}

and
{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\Gamma}
is a quasi-preimage of
{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\Delta}
.
In that case, we call
\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{F}
a
quasi-preimage
of
\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{\varphi}
.

Finally, we call a validity judgement
{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\Delta}\vdash_{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\overline{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}T}}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}}\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{\varphi}
in HOL
proper
iff

1.

{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\Delta}
is proper,

2.

\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{\varphi}
is quasi-proper in context
{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\Delta}

In this case, we will call
{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\overline{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\Gamma}\color[rgb]{1,0,0}}\vdash_{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\overline{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}T}}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}}\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\overline{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{F}}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}
a relativization of
{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\Delta}\vdash_{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\overline{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}T}}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}}\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{\varphi}
and
{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\Gamma}\mathbin{\vdash_{{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}T}}}\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{F}
a
quasi-preimage
of the statement
{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\Delta}\vdash_{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\overline{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}T}}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}}\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{\varphi}
, where
{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\Gamma}
is a quasi-preimage of
{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\Delta}
and
\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{F}
a quasi-preimage of
\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{\varphi}
.
Additionally, for HOL terms with preimages we consider these preimages to be quasi-preimages of the HOL term as well.

0.D.3
Transforming HOL derivations into admissible HOL derivations

It will be useful to distinguish between two different kinds of improper terms.

Definition 7

An improper term is called
almost proper
iff its normalization isn’t spurious (w.r.t. a given quasi-preimage) and contains no spurious subterms, otherwise it is said to be
unnormalizably spurious
.
This means that improper terms are almost proper iff their given quasi-preimage is well-typed.
Since proper terms have well-typed preimages, they are almost proper (w.r.t. this preimage) as well.

In order to lift a HOL derivation to DHOL, we first have to choose (quasi)-preimage types for all term occuring in it (at which point we use the notions of spurious and almost proper terms w.r.t. these DHOL types).

Choosing (quasi-)preimage types for a HOL derivation

Lemma 6
(Indexing lemma)

Assume that
{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\Gamma}\mathbin{\vdash_{{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}T}}}\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{F}:{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathsf{bool}}
holds in DHOL. Given a valid HOL derivation
D
of the statement
{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\overline{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\Gamma}\color[rgb]{1,0,0}}\vdash_{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\overline{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}T}}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}}\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\overline{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{F}}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}
, we can choose a DHOL type
T(\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{t})
(called type index) for each occurence of a HOL term
\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{t}
in
D
, s.t. the following properties hold:

1.

T(\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\overline{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{t}}\color[rgb]{0,0,0})=\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.5}{A^{\prime}}
with
\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\overline{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.5}{A}}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}=\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\overline{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.5}{A^{\prime}}}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}
for any DHOL term
\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{t}
satisfying
\Gamma\mathbin{\vdash_{{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}T}}}\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{t}:\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.5}{A}
,

2.

T({\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\mathtt{c}})=\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.5}{A}
if
{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\mathtt{c}}:\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.5}{A}
is a constant in
{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}T}
,

3.

T({\color[rgb]{0,0,1}x})=\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.5}{A}
if
{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}x}:\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.5}{A}
is variable declaration in
{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\Gamma}
,

4.

T(\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{s})=T(\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{t})
for
\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{s},\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{t}
within an equality of the form
\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{s}\,{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathsf{=}}_{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.5}{A}}\,\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{t}
for some HOL type
\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.5}{A}
,

5.

T(\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{s})=T(\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{t})={\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathsf{bool}}
for
\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{s},\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{t}
within an implication of the form
\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{s}\Rightarrow\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{t}
,

6.

T({\color[rgb]{0,0,1}x})=\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.5}{A}
for
{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}x}
in
\left(\lambda{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}x}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathsf{\mathord{:}}}\mathbb{B}.~\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{s}\right)\ \color[rgb]{0,0,1}{t}
if
T(\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{t})=\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.5}{A}
,

7.

T(\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{s}\,{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathsf{=}}_{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.5}{A}}\,\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{t})={\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathsf{bool}}
,

8.

when variables are moved from the context into a
\lambda
-binder or vice versa the index of said variable is preserved

9.

whenever a term
\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{t}
occurs both in the assumptions and conclusions of a step
S
in
D
, the index of
\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{t}
is the same all those occurrences of
\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{t}
in
S
,

10.

if
{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}x},\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{t}
in
\lambda{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}x}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathsf{\mathord{:}}}\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\overline{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.5}{B}}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}.~\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{t}
satisfy
T({\color[rgb]{0,0,1}x})=\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.5}{A}
and
T(\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{t})=\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.5}{B}
, then
T(\lambda{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}x}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathsf{\mathord{:}}}\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.5}{B}.~\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{t})={\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathsf{\Pi}}{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}x}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathsf{\mathord{:}}}\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.5}{A}.~\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.5}{B}
,

11.

T(\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{t})=\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.5}{A}
implies
\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{t}
has type
\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\overline{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.5}{A}}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}
and
\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.5}{A}
is non-empty.

Proof
(Proof by induction of the shape of
D
)

This lemma only holds for well-formed derivations of translations of well-typed conjectures over well-formed theories. It will not hold for arbitrary formulae (as can be seen by considering equalities between constants of equal HOL but different DHOL types). The proof of the lemma will therefore use that fact that the final statement in the derivation is the translation of a well-typed DHOL statement (which already determines the ”correct” indices for the terms within that statement) and then show that for each step in a well-formed HOL proof concluding a statement that we can correctly index, the assumptions of that step can also be indexed correctly. We will thus proceed by ”backwards induction” on the shape of the derivation
D
.

The assumptions of the theory and contexthood rules only contain terms already contained in the conclusions, so the associated cases in the proof are all trivial.

Similarly the assumptions of lookup rules, type well-formedness and type equality rules contain no additional terms, so those cases are also trivial.

The typing rules are about forming larger terms from subterms, so if those larger terms can be consistently (i.e. according to the claim of the lemma) indexed, then the same is necessarily also true for the subterms. Thus the typing rules also have only trivial cases.
By the same arguments the cases for the congruence rules (
), (
), the symmetry and transitivity rules for term equality and the rules (
), (
), (
) and (
) are also all trivial.

It remains to consider the cases for the rules (
), (
), (
) and (
).

Regarding (
):

Here the assumptions of the rule contain the additional terms
\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{F}
and
\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{F}\Rightarrow\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{G}
. However as both terms are of type
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathsf{bool}}
all their (quasi)-preimages have type
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathsf{bool}}
as well and picking indices according to any of the quasi-preimages of
\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{F}\Rightarrow\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{G}
will work.

Regarding (
):

Here the assumptions of the rule contain the additional terms
\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{F^{\prime}}
and
\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{F}\,{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathsf{=}}_{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathsf{bool}}}\,\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{F^{\prime}}
. Both terms are of type
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathsf{bool}}
, so by the same argument as in the previous case, we can index them consistently with the claim of this lemma.

Regarding (
):

Here the assumptions of the rule contain the additional terms
\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{p}\ {\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathsf{true}}
and
\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{p}\ {\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathsf{false}}
and
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathsf{true}}
and
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathsf{false}}
. All these terms are of type
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathsf{bool}}
, so by the same argument as in the previous two cases, we can index them consistently with the claim of this lemma.

Regarding (
):

Here the second assumption contains an additional variable of type
\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.5}{A}
. As this variable doesn’t occur in any other terms, we can index it by the type of any of its (quasi)-preimages.
By assumption, the DHOL theory is partially inhabited, so we can chose this type index to be inhabited.

Remark 2

In the following, we will use the term
type index
to refer to a choice of DHOL types for each HOL term in a derivation satisfying the properties of the previous lemma.

Transforming unnormalizably spurious terms into almost proper terms in HOL derivations

Definition 8

A valid HOL derivation is called
admissible
iff we can choose quasi-preimages for all terms occurring in it s.t. all terms in the derivation are almost proper w.r.t. their chosen quasi-preimages.

This definition is useful, since admissible derivations are precisely those HOL derivations that allow us to consistently lift the terms occurring in them to well-typed DHOL terms.

In the following, we describe a proof transformation which maps HOL derivations to admissible HOL derivations.

Definition 9

A
statement transformation
in a given logic is a map that maps statements in the logic to statements in the logic.
Similarly an
indexed statement transformation
is a map that maps HOL statements with indexed terms to HOL statements.

Definition 10

A
macro-step

M

for
an (indexed) statement transformation
T

replacing
a step
S
in a derivation is a sequence of steps
S_{1},\ldots,S_{n}
(called
micro-steps
of
M
) s.t. the assumptions of the
S_{i}
that are not concluded by
S_{j}
with
j<i
are results of applying
T
to assumptions of step
S
and furthermore the conclusion of step
S_{n}
is the result of applying
T
to the conclusion of
S
. The assumptions of those
S_{j}
that are not concluded by previous micro-steps of
M
are called the
assumptions of macro-step

M
and the conclusion of the last micro-step
S_{n}
of
M
is called the
conclusion of macro-step

M
.

Thus we we can replace each step in a derivation by a macro step replacing that step, we can transform that derivation to a derivation in which the given indexed statement transformation is applied to all statements. This is useful to simplify and normalize derivations to derivations with certain additional properties.
In our case, we want to normalize a given HOL derivation into an admissible HOL derivation. Thus, we need to define an indexed statement transformation for which all terms in the image of the transformation are almost proper and the replace all steps in the derivation by macro steps for that statement transformation.

Since the notion of a quasi-proper term only makes sense once we fix a choice of type indices (in the sense of Lemma
6
), the indexed statement transformation will actually depend on the choice of type indices.

The idea for our transformation is that given a choice of type indices according to Lemma
6
, the only remaining ”source” of spuriousness are ill-typed function applications. There are two cases of such function applications, either they are applications that can be beta reduced to non-spurious terms or they can not. As it turns out in the second case the proof cannot meaningfully depend on that term, so we can simply replace it with a proper one. The below definition simply spells this out in detail.

Definition 11

A
normalizing statement transformation

\mathsf{sRed}\left(\cdot\right)
is defined to be an indexed statement transformation that replaces terms in statements as described below.
The definition of the transformation of a term depends on its type index — a DHOL-type
\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.5}{A}
(called
preimage type
) — for each term
\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{t}
.
We will write those types as indices to the HOL terms, so for instance
\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{t}_{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.5}{A}}
indicates a HOL term
\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{t}
of type
\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\overline{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.5}{A}}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}
and preimage type
\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.5}{A}
.

These preimage types are used to effectively associate to each term a type of a possible quasi-preimage (hence their name), which is useful as for
\lambda
-functions there are quasi-preimages of potentially many different types.
We require that for an indexed term
\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{t}_{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.5}{A}}
, term
\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{t}
has type
\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\overline{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.5}{A}}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}
and that for almost proper terms
\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{t}_{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.5}{A}}
with unique quasi-preimage the quasi-preimage has type
\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.5}{A}
.

Since variables and lambda binders are the sole cause for HOL terms having multiple quasi-preimages, choosing indices for variables in HOL terms induces unique quasi-preimages (respecting those type indices). This uniqueness is a direct consequence of (the proof of) Lemma
5
.

We will consider only those quasi-preimages that respect type indices, for the notions of unnormalizably spurious and almost proper terms.

With respect to these choices, the transformation will do the following two things (in this order) in order to ”normalize” unnormalizably spurious terms to almost proper ones:

1.

apply beta and eta reductions and in case this doesn’t yield almost proper terms

2.

replace unnormalizably spurious function applications of type
\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.5}{B}
by the ”default terms”
\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{w}_{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.5}{B}}
of type
\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\overline{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.5}{B}}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}
, defined as the translation of a fixed DHOL term of type
\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.5}{B}
(whose existence follows from the last property of Lemma
6
).

As we are assuming a valid HOL derivation indexed according to Lemma
6
, we will only define this transformation on well-typed HOL terms with preimage types consistent with the indexing lemma.
We can then define the transformation of
\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{t}_{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.5}{A}}
(denoted by
\mathsf{sRed}\left(\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{t}_{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.5}{A}}\right)
) by first matching line for
\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{t}_{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.5}{A}}
as follows:

\displaystyle\mathsf{sRed}\left(\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{t}_{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.5}{A}}\right)

\displaystyle:=

\displaystyle\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{t}_{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.5}{A}}

if
\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{t}
has quasi-preimage of type
\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.5}{A}

(SR1)

\displaystyle\mathsf{sRed}\left(\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{f}_{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathsf{\Pi}}{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}x}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathsf{\mathord{:}}}\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.5}{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.5}{A}}.~\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.5}{B}}\ \color[rgb]{0,0,1}{t}_{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.5}{A}}\right)\!\!\!\!\!\!

isn’t almost proper with a quasi-preimage of type

\displaystyle\mathsf{sRed}\left(\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{s}_{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.5}{A}}\,{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathsf{=}}_{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\overline{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.5}{A}}\color[rgb]{0,0,1}}\,\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{t}_{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.5}{A^{\prime}}}\right)

\mathsf{sRed}\left(\mathsf{sRed}\left(\cdot\right)\right)

\mathsf{sRed}\left(\cdot\right)
.

Lemma 7

Assume a well-typed DHOL theory
T
and a conjecture
{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\Gamma}\mathbin{\vdash_{{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}T}}}\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{\varphi}
with
{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\Gamma}
well-formed and
\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{\varphi}
well-typed. Assume a valid HOL derivation
D
of
{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\overline{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\Gamma}\color[rgb]{1,0,0}}\vdash_{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\overline{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}T}}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}}\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\overline{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\varphi}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}
.
Choose type indices for the terms in
D
according to the properties of the indexing lemma (Lemma
6
).
Then, for any steps
S
in
D
we can construct a macro-step for the normalizing statement transformation replacing step
S
s.t. after replacing all steps by their macro-steps:

•

the resulting derivation is valid,

•

all terms occurring in the derivation are almost proper (w.r.t. the quasi-preimages determined by the type indices).

Proof
(Proof of Lemma
7
)

We will show this by induction on the shape of
D
.

Firstly, we observe that there are no dependent types in HOL and the context and axioms contain no spurious subterms. Hence, well-formedness (of theories, contexts, types) and type-equality judgements are unaffected by the transformation.
So there is nothing to prove for the well-formedness and type-equality rules (those steps can be replaced by a macro step containing exactly this single step).

Regarding the type indices:

We observe that the properties of the type indices provided by Lemma
6
ensure that the smallest unnormalizably spurious terms (i.e. without unnormalizably spurious proper subterms) are function applications in which function and argument are both almost proper. Furthermore, in such a case the function is not a
\lambda
-function.

It remains to consider the typing and validity rules and to construct macro steps for the steps in the derivation using them for the normalizing statement transformation.

Since terms indexed by a type
\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.5}{A}
have type
\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\overline{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.5}{A}}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}
it is easy to see from Definition
11
that the normalizing statement transformation replaces terms of type
\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\overline{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.5}{A}}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}
by terms of type
\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\overline{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.5}{A}}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}
.

(
):

Since constants are proper terms, there is nothing to prove.

(
):

Since context variables are proper terms, there is nothing to prove.

(
):

\displaystyle{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\Delta}\vdash_{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\overline{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}T}}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}}

\displaystyle\mathsf{sRed}\left(\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{s}\right)_{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.5}{A}}\mathord{:}\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\overline{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.5}{A}}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}

0.D.4
Lifting admissible HOL derivations of validity statements to DHOL

We finally have all required results to prove the soundness of the translation from DHOL to HOL.

Proof
(Proof of Theorem
7.2
)

As shown in Lemma
7
, we may assume that the proof of
{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\overline{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\Gamma}\color[rgb]{1,0,0}}\vdash_{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\overline{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}T}}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}}\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\overline{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{F}}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}
is admissible, so it only contains almost-proper terms.
Consequently, whenever an equality
\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{s}\,{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathsf{=}}_{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\overline{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.5}{A}}\color[rgb]{0,0,1}}\,\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{t}
is derivable in HOL and
\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{s^{\prime}},\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{t^{\prime}}
are the quasi-preimages of
\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{s},\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{t}
respectively, it follows that it’s quasi-preimage
\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{s^{\prime}}\,{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathsf{=}}_{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.5}{A}}\,\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{t}
is well-typed in DHOL and thus
\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{s^{\prime}}\mathord{:}\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.5}{A}
and
\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{t^{\prime}}\mathord{:}\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.5}{A}
.
Without loss of generality (adding extra assumptions throughout the proof) we may assume that the context of the (final) conclusion is the translation of a DHOL context.
By Lemma
5
the translation is term-wise injective.

Therefore, the translated conjecture is a proper validity statement with unique (quasi)-preimage in DHOL.
If we can lift a derivation of the translated conjecture to a valid DHOL derivation of its quasi-preimage, the resulting derivation is a valid derivation of the original conjecture.
This means, that it suffices to prove that we can lift admissible derivations of a proper validity statement
S
in HOL to a derivation of a quasi-preimage of
S
.

We prove this claim by induction on the validity rules of HOL as follows:

Given a validity rule
R
with assumptions
A_{1},\ldots,A_{n}
, validity assumptions (assumptions that are validity statements)
V_{1},\ldots,V_{m}
, non-judgement assumptions (meaning assumptions that something occurs in a context or theory)
N_{1},\ldots,N_{p}
and conclusion
C
we will show the following:

Claim

Assuming that the
A_{i}
and the
N_{j}
hold.

1.

Assume that the conclusion
C
is proper with quasi-preimage
C^{-1}
. Then the contexts
C_{i}
of the
V_{i}
are proper and the quasi-preimages of the
V_{i}
are well-formed.

2.

Assume that whenever an
V_{i}
is proper its quasi-preimage (where we choose the same preimages for identical terms and types with several possible preimages) holds in DHOL and that the conclusion
C
is proper with quasi-preimage
C^{-1}
.
Then,
C^{-1}
holds in DHOL.

Consider the first part of this claim, namely that if
C
is proper then the
V_{i}
are proper. Since all formulae appearing in the derivation are almost proper, this implies that the
V_{i}
themselves are proper and by construction (choice of quasi-preimage) the contexts of their quasi-preimages fit together with the context of
C^{-1}
.

The translation clearly implies that if an
N_{j}
holds in HOL, the corresponding non-judgement assumption
{N_{j}}^{-1}
holds in DHOL (e.g. if
\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\overline{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{F}}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}
is an axiom in
\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\overline{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}T}}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}
, then
\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{F}
must be an axiom in
{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}T}
).

Since the validity judgement being derived is proper, it follows from this first part of the claim that the validity assumptions of all validity rules in the derivation are proper.

By induction on the validity rules, if given an arbitrary validity rule
R
whose assumptions hold and whose validity assumptions all satisfy a property
P
we can show that
P
holds on the conclusion of
R
, then all derivable validity judgments have property
P
.
Since all the validity assumptions and conclusions of validity rules in the derivation are proper, the property of having a derivable quasi-preimage is such a property.
By this induction principle, it suffices to prove the claim for the validity rules in HOL.

We will therefore consider the validity rules one by one. For each rule we first prove the first part of the claim.
Sometimes we also need that the quasi-preimages of some non-validity (typically typing) assumptions hold, so we will prove that this also follows from the conclusion being proper.
Then the assumption of the second part, combined with the first part implies that the quasi-preimages of the
V_{i}
hold in DHOL and it is easy to prove that also
C^{-1}
holds in DHOL.

Throughout this proof we will use the notation

\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{t}

\textstyle\kern-0.6pt\sim\kern-0.6pt

to denote that
\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{t}
is some quasi-preimage of

\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{t}

\textstyle\kern-0.6pt\sim\kern-0.6pt

.
Since the translation is surjective on type-level we will only need this notation on term-level.

Validity can be shown using the rules (

For the second part, we still need the quasi-preimage of the assumption to hold, so we will show that it follows from the conclusion being proper.

Since the conclusion is proper, it follows that the preimage

{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\Gamma}\mathbin{\vdash_{{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}T}}}\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{f}\,{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathsf{=}}_{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathsf{\Pi}}{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}x}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathsf{\mathord{:}}}\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.5}{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.5}{A}}.~\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.5}{B}}\,\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{f^{\prime}}
are quasi-preimages of the validity assumptions.

Since the validity assumptions use the same context as the conclusion, it follows that they are both proper with uniquely determined context. As observed in the beginning of the proof if a proper assumption of a rule is an equality over a type
\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\overline{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.5}{A}}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}
, the induction hypothesis implies that the quasi-preimage of that assumption in which the equality is over type
\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.5}{A}
must be well-formed.
Hence both
{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\Gamma}\mathbin{\vdash_{{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}T}}}\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{t}\,{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathsf{=}}_{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.5}{A}}\,\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{t^{\prime}}
and
{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\Gamma}\mathbin{\vdash_{{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}T}}}\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{f}\,{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathsf{=}}_{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathsf{\Pi}}{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}x}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathsf{\mathord{:}}}\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.5}{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.5}{A}}.~\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.5}{B}}\,\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{f^{\prime}}
are well-formed in DHOL, so we have proven the first part of the claim.

Regarding the second part of the claim:

\displaystyle{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\Gamma}\mathbin{\vdash_{{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}T}}}

\displaystyle\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{t}\,{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathsf{=}}_{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.5}{A}}\,\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{t^{\prime}}

By Assumption

is well-typed.
